Robust ranking of renewable energy alternatives handling uncertainty using novel hesitant bi-fuzzy MEREC-MOORA and Dombi aggregation approach
Authors: Bahnisikha Roy Muhuri, B. S. Mahapatra, G. S. Mahapatra, Debashis Ghosh, Prabhjot Kaur, Arti Choudhary
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The identification of competitive renewable energy (RE) sources often fails to account for non-technical factors, such as environmental, political, and social barriers. These considerations of RE are described in language that accommodates varying levels of hesitancy among decision-makers (DMs). This study develops a multi-criteria group decision-making (MCGDM) method using hesitant bi-fuzzy sets to capture DMs’ varying degrees of hesitancy. A HBF extension of multi-objective optimization on the basis of ratio analysis is proposed, incorporating a method based on the removal effect on the criteria to assign parameter importance for each DM. This study also developed a novel HBF-Dombi aggregating operator for logical synthesis of qualitative data. The developed MCGDM framework is applied to determine the optimal RE sources, considering 5 DMs, 6 RE sources, and 15 barriers classified into 5 categories. Solar energy emerges as the top choice since it is favorable in both technical and non-technical RE barriers. A comparative analysis is performed to validate the proposed method, while comprehensive sensitivity analyses are performed to assess the influence of parameter variation on the ranking of RE sources. The insights from this article can serve as a pathway for identifying RE barriers to successful RE installation.

Introduction

The modern world is experiencing considerable changes in climate due to global warming from industrial development, urbanization, agriculture, and the greenhouse effect1. The power demand in India is rapidly growing with the rising urbanization, industrialization, and mobility of electric vehicles. In India, 56.2% of electricity is generated through fossil fuels, most of which are imported, burdening the economy2. India is forecast to generate roughly 62% of its energy demand from non-fossil sources by 2030, about 12% higher than the target. The growth rate of renewable energy (RE) generation in India is approximately 8.29%, which is significantly low compared to European countries3. India is naturally endowed with extensive resources for RE. Hence, a vast scope exists to control carbon emissions by reducing fuel-based electricity. In addition, the integration of renewable energy sources with regenerative organic farming can reduce the reliance on fossil fuels, thereby reducing greenhouse gas emissions and promoting sustainability4. Solar, hydro, and wind power are the most significant energy suppliers among RE options in India, while biomass, tidal, and geothermal power are presently in the nascent phases of development5.

Even though RE sources are limitless, they are not easy to utilize and fully deploy nationwide6. Furthermore, the energy supply is inconsistent due to the unpredictable nature of specific energy sources. As a result, assessing these sources requires careful evaluation of important characteristics. The multifaceted nature of this collaborative decision-making process necessitates careful consideration of various technical, corporate, financial, political, and environmental implications7. Choosing the best RE source will minimize the adverse effects of environmental stressors and climate change while fostering the nation’s financial growth8. We aim to discern India’s most suitable RE sources, evaluated through five primary factors and fifteen sub-factors. Nevertheless, qualitative evaluations of RE implementation hurdles are not quantified by the fundamental Multi-Criteria Decision-Making (MCDM) paradigm. Consequently, developing a sophisticated group decision approach for handling qualitative data is more effective in identifying the primary obstacles in the RE area.

An important development to accurately represent hesitation and indecisiveness in decision-making is hesitant fuzzy sets (HFSs)9,10. HFSs let multiple membership functions represent uncertainty, including hesitation. Though HFS provides a more nuanced way of representing uncertainty and imprecision, it has a limitation in that it does not consider multiple grades of non-membership. Zhu et al.11 improved the HFS by introducing another membership degree to develop the dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFSs). The DHFSs serve as a more fitting framework for articulating the indecision of the decision-maker (DM) regarding support and contradiction, as they facilitate the concurrent evaluation of various levels about both categories. Because DHFS treated the membership functions independently, membership grades often yield unrealistic values. Chaube et al.12 addressed this restriction by presenting hesitant bi-fuzzy sets (HBFSs), which may allow any degree of hesitation in membership and non-membership while maintaining their relationship, i.e., the total of any two membership grades is less than one. The HBFS is based on the idea that many pairs of membership functions show how people usually make decisions: stronger support means lesser opposition. As a result, HBFSs are better suited to express complex decision-making processes that incorporate reluctance in experts’ judgments via membership grades.

It is critical to aggregate the acquired data in a multi-criteria group decision-making (MCGDM) issue in a credible process that ensures the originality of the expert’s assessments13. The aggregation of fuzziness in an MCGDM issue involves two scenarios: the incorporation of DM’s preferences alongside their assessments of the criteria to create a combined assessment and the accumulation of criteria weights with the aggregated assessments of the DMs to yield a weighted combined assessment14. The weighting procedure is essential for any MCGDM problem, ensuring the computation process maintains the integrity of the qualitative data collected15. However, most researchers reduce the qualitative aspects to mitigate computational complexities. Hence, it is essential to develop an aggregation operator based on HBFS to integrate the bipolar information16 of qualitative assessment to keep the computation procedure logical and acceptable.

This study identifies the perspective research gaps from the current studies as follows:Prioritization of non-technical criteria such as corporate culture and priorities, risk-return trade-offs, public perceptions and awareness, international commerce, global issues, and environmental factors such as habitat disruption to identify appropriate RE sources frequently overlooked in the previous studies.It is crucial to maintain similarity in the computation process by logically synthesizing HBF information into an MCGDM problem. The Dombi weighted-average operator17 can be employed for this purpose; however, it has not yet been established in the HBF context in the literature.The current literature needs a more convenient MCGDM methodology that integrates assessment of criteria through the HBF measure, determination of objective criteria preference, and credibility assessment of DMs.The existing literature sometimes fails to adequately address the individuality of the DM’s perspective in determining the criteria weight. This individuality is crucial for evaluating the criteria ratings and their associated preferences.The article’s motivation is derived from the proposal’s perspective, which involves the integration of non-technical parameters in the selection of RE sources to promote environmental sustainability, the establishment of a novel operator for the logical integration of hesitant data in MCGDM problems, and the development of an MCGDM framework for the determination of a reliable ranking of the alternatives. This framework should be primarily data-driven to reduce subjectivity when dealing with varying degrees of hesitancy.

Concurrently, it also considers non-technical factors such as corporate culture, water and sound pollution, and habitat disruption, assigning them appropriate significance.

A novel extension of the Dombi operator is proposed for aggregating uncertain bipolar information in the context of MCGDM. The proposed HBF-Dombi operator enables amalgamation of two HBFSs for constructing a weighted HBF-decision matrix, thereby maintaining the hesitancy in the procedure.

A significant contribution of this study lies in applying the method based on the removal effects of criteria (MEREC)18 method to determine the weights assigned to each criterion for each DM. This approach facilitates a more candid exchange of viewpoints among decision-making experts; it refrains from enforcing any predetermined metrics. Furthermore, the individuality in GDM is maintained for a satisfactory duration, ensuring that each decision-making aspect is significant within the GDM process, thereby enriching individual perspectives in GDM19.

Furthermore, the individuality in GDM is maintained for a satisfactory duration, ensuring that each decision-making aspect is significant within the GDM process, thereby enriching individual perspectives in GDM19.

The extension of the multi-objective optimization on the basis of ratio analysis (MOORA) in the HBF environment facilitates the determination of a sequence of alternatives while preserving hesitancy in the computation process until the last phase. The suggested HBF-MOORA20 is implemented to evaluate the RE sources and pinpoint the critical factors that underlie the obstacles to their implementation.

Literature review

This section delves into the significance of the Dombi operator in aggregating uncertain information, the fuzzy MOORA methodology in qualitative decision-making contexts, and HBF decision-making frameworks designed to address RE challenges.

Dombi operator and its requirement in MCGDM

Dombi21 introduced new operations using t-norm to represent AND functions and t-conorm to represent OR functions. The Dombi norm’s main advantage is that it is parameter-dependent, and as the parameter varies, so does the resulting norm. The fuzzy extension of the Dombi operator strengthened the aggregation of uncertain information to maintain logical similarity in decision-making problems22. Several fuzzy set theories have recently been employed to solve decision-making problems using the Dombi operator, aiming to improve the accuracy of the results by managing uncertainty. Lu and Ye23 addressed the Dombi operator for cubic linguistic variables for solving an MCDM problem of hiring the best software engineer for a company. A combination of Dombi operators and spherical fuzzy sets is employed to address a GDM issue in selecting the optimal investment for a financial management organization24. The fermatean extension expanded the Dombi operator’s utility in MCGDM to identify the most suitable location through the Technique for Order of Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution (TOPSIS) approach25. Another development of the Dombi operator is the extension to circular q-Rung ortho-pair fuzzy sets, enabling the application of symmetry analysis using artificial intelligence26. A Pythagorean extension of the Dombi operator addresses the decision-making problem of identifying a specific expert in the medical domain27. A picture fuzzy extension of the Dombi operator was proposed by Hussain et al.28 to determine the appropriate recycling machines for waste materials. Saha et al.29 proposed a Fermatean extension of Dombi operators and applied it to an MCGDM problem of identifying technology for material handling. Rong and Yu30 developed a q-rof picture fuzzy Dombi operator for an MCGDM supplier selection problem. Javed et al.31 established a T-spherical fuzzy Dombi operator for road construction company selection. An intuitionistic linguistic Dombi operator was developed by Ali et al.32 for the selection of artificial intelligence-based movable devices. Meher and Alrasheedi33 proposed trapezoidal-valued intuitionistic Dombi operators to solve the MCGDM issue of photovoltaic site selection. These studies highlight the operator’s adaptability and resilience across various MCGDM frameworks. These discussions lead us to believe that the HBF extension of the Dombi operator may effectively portray the probable and questionable data that arise in real-world problems. However, the existing studies reveal that the Dombi aggregation operator has not been extended to HBFs, which could be advantageous for aggregating bipolar uncertain information in MCDM issues.

Fuzzy extension of MOORA approach

The MOORA approach34 addresses an MCDM problem by converting the decision matrix into an objective through ratio analysis. The classical variant of the MOORA approach has two primary limitations: it cannot ascertain criterion weights, and it cannot accommodate qualitative assessments. The simplicity of the MOORA approach attracts researchers to enhance it by combining it with various extensions of fuzzy sets, thereby addressing the challenges of uncertain decision-making. Haiyun et al.35 introduced an interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy Decision-Making Trial and Evaluation Laboratory (DEMATEL)-MOORA methodology aimed at discerning customer expectations, which is considered the paramount strategy for implementing green supply chains within the energy sector. Shahzadi et al.36 developed a Fermatean fuzzy MOORA methodology to address challenges in intelligent manufacturing systems. Mota et al.37 introduced a comprehensive fuzzy BWM-MOORA methodology for assessing the health of investment firms. The study by Khorshidi et al.38 employed a fuzzy DEMATEL-MOORA methodology to identify suitable locations for solar plants. The same methodology has been developed by Ecer et al.39 using spherical fuzzy to determine the appropriate smart collars to assist farmers. Kou et al.40 applied an extended picture fuzzy MOORA approach to examine carbon footprint-based investments. Mahapatra et al.13 developed an MCGDM approach with a Pythagorean information-based MOORA approach for finding suitable ERP software. Magableh et al.7 presented a fuzzy SAW-MOORA framework to assess sustainable RE plans. The enhanced MOORA approach is available, but it is too limited as a combined methodology for weight allocation and qualitative assessment to rank the alternatives.

Decision-making under uncertainty in RE sector

The integration of MCDM into fuzzy modeling of uncertainty offers a systematic approach to analyzing the RE sources by incorporating the uncertainty and hesitation into both qualitative and quantitative descriptions of the RE parameters41. Krishankumar et al.42 proposed an integrated Choquet integral and CRiteria Importance Through Intercriteria Correlation (CRITIC) for RE source selection for smart cities. Narayanamoorthy et al.43 proposed linear diophantine HFS to access India’s energy reserve capacities through the MARICA method. To evaluate energy storage technologies, Liang et al.44 proposed a dual-parameter-based TOPSIS method with multigranular, unbalanced hesitation data. In their work, Su et al.45 established an MCGDM framework that utilized hesitant probabilistic fuzzy sets, along with social network analysis, to identify an appropriate RE scheme. Seikh and Chatterjee46 presented the Fermatean fuzzy SWARA-ARAS method to establish an MCGDM framework for assessing RE resources. Gupta et al.10 incorporates HBF information into the TOPSIS approach to construct an MCGDM approach for RE system selection. A fuzzy MULTIMOORA approach is introduced by Shamsipour et al.47 to perform a case study on RE source selection. The assessment of RE sources takes into account investment purposes48 and financial risk management via machine learning49. Although MCDM and its fuzzy extensions can identify viable RE sources50–52, a better MCGDM technique is still necessary to capture the ambiguity and reluctance linked to RE source selection. In this context, a cautious fuzzy extension of the MCGDM technique may play an important role in accepting varied levels of uncertainty in the RE sector and improving the process.

Proposed HBF Dombi arithmetic aggression operator

In this section, the HBF Dombi operator is introduced after reviewing the fundamentals of HFSs.

Definition 5

Defuzzification of CBFE12: The score function of a CBFE, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=(\digamma _{C},~\Delta _{C})$$\end{document} is transformed to a crisp value using Eq. (2).2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S(C)=\frac{\digamma _{C}-\Delta _{C}+1}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Definition 5 transforms CBFE information to a crisp value. The difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\digamma _{C}-\Delta _{C}$$\end{document} serves as a net evaluation measure, capturing the dominance of the membership information over the uncertainty component. By adding the constant 1 and dividing by 2, this value is normalized to the interval [0, 1], preserving the ranking of alternatives and facilitating consistent comparisons in MCDM.

Definition 7

Proposed HBF Dombi operator: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{1}=(\gamma _{1},~\eta _{1})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{2}=(\gamma _{2},~\eta _{2})$$\end{document} be two HBFEs and Λ >0. \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \mathscr {D}_{1}\bigoplus \mathscr {D}_{2}=\left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{\left( \frac{{\gamma _{1}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _1}^2}\right) ^k+\left( \frac{{\gamma _{2}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _2}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\left( \frac{{\eta _1}^2}{1-{\eta _{1}}^{2}}\right) ^k+\left( \frac{{\eta _2}^2}{1-{{\eta _{2}}^{2}}}\right) ^k}\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \mathscr {D}_{1}\bigotimes \mathscr {D}_2=\left( \sqrt{\frac{1}{{1+\{\left( \frac{1-{\gamma _1}^2}{{\gamma _{1}}^{2}}\right) ^k+\left( \frac{1-{\gamma _2}^2}{{\gamma _{2}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}},\sqrt{1-{{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\left( \frac{1-{\eta _{1}}^{2}}{{\eta _1}^2}\right) ^k+\left( \frac{1-{{\eta _{2}}^{2}}}{{\eta _2}^2}\right) ^k}\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \Lambda \mathscr {D}_{1}=\left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{\Lambda \left( \frac{{\gamma _{1}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _1}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\Lambda {\left( \frac{{\eta _1}^2}{1-{\eta _{1}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\mathscr {D}_{1}}^{\Lambda }=\left( \sqrt{\frac{1}{{1+\{\Lambda \left( \frac{1-{\gamma _1}^2}{{\gamma _{1}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}},\sqrt{1-{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\Lambda {\left( \frac{1-{\eta _{1}}^{2}}{{\eta _1}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

The proposed HBF Dombi operator is primarily constructed by applying the classical concept of the Dombi t-norm and t-conorm, extending it to the HBF framework. This operator aggregates membership and non-membership degrees while maintaining the essential constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \gamma + \eta \le 1$$\end{document}. It satisfies fundamental properties such as closure, commutativity, monotonicity, and boundedness. The constant Λ controls the flexibility of the aggregation behavior, allowing the modeling of various decision-making environments. Thus, the proposed HBF Dombi operator offers a flexible framework for aggregating HBF information.

Theorem 1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}=(\gamma ,~\eta )$$\end{document} be HBFE, then hesitant bi-fuzzy Dombi weighted average (HBFDWA) operator is a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$HBFDWA:~{HBFE}^n\rightarrow {HBFE}$$\end{document} such that7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{n}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi =(\psi _{1},~\psi _{2},\ldots ,~\psi _{n})^T$$\end{document} be weight vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{t}>0~ \forall ~t$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{t=1}^{n}{\psi _{t}}=1$$\end{document}

Proof

The demonstration of Theorem 1 is self-evident. □

Theorem 2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}=(\gamma ,~\eta )$$\end{document} be HBFE, then HBFDWA operator is a function HBFDWA : HBFEn → HBFE such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,\mathscr {D}_{n})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{n}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})=\\ & \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{n}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{n}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi =(\psi _{1},~\psi _{2},\ldots ,\psi _{n})^T$$\end{document} be the weight vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{t}>0~\forall ~t$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{t=1}^{n}{\psi _{t}}=1$$\end{document}.

Proof

From Theorem 1, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_2)=({\psi _1}{\mathscr {D}_{1}}\bigoplus {\psi _2}{\mathscr {D}_2})$$\end{document} for t=1. Using Eqs. (3) and (5), we have8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\psi _1}{\mathscr {D}_{1}}\bigoplus {\psi _2}{\mathscr {D}_2}= \\ & \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{\psi _1\left( \frac{{\gamma _{1}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _1}^2}\right) ^k+\psi _2\left( \frac{{\gamma _{2}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _2}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\psi _1{\left( \frac{{\eta _1}^2}{1-{\eta _{1}}^{2}}\right) ^k+\psi _2\left( \frac{{\eta _2}^2}{1-{{\eta _{2}}^{2}}}\right) ^k}\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right) \nonumber \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Consequently, Theorem 2 is valid for t=2. Let us suppose that Theorem 2 is valid for t=m. Then, we have9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{m})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{m}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})\\ &= \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \nonumber \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So, for t=m+1, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{m},~ \mathscr {D}_{m+1})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{m+1}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{m}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})\bigoplus {{\psi _{m+1}}\mathscr {D}_{m+1}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}According to Eqs. (8) and (9),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bigoplus _{t=1}^{m+1}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})\bigoplus {\mathscr {D}_{m+1}}=\left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m+1}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m+1}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Consequently, mathematical induction establishes Theorem 2 for any natural number. □

Theorem 3

Idempotent Property: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}=(\gamma _{t},~\eta _{t})$$\end{document}. for t=1(1)n be HBFEs that are equivalent, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}=\mathscr {D}~\forall ~t$$\end{document}. Following this, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})=\mathscr {D} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

From Theorem 2, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{n}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})=\\ & \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{n}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{n}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi =(\psi _{1},~\psi _{2},\ldots ,~\psi _{n})^T$$\end{document} be weight vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{t}>0~ \forall ~t$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{t=1}^{n}{\psi _{t}}=1$$\end{document}. Now, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}=\mathscr {D}~ \forall ~t$$\end{document}, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bigoplus _{t=1}^{n}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})= \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{\left( \frac{{\gamma }^{2}}{1-{\gamma }^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\left( \frac{{\eta }^2}{1-{\eta }^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{n}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})=\mathscr {D}$$\end{document}. □

Theorem 4

Boundary Property: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}=(\gamma _{t},~\eta _{t})$$\end{document} for t=1,~2,… ,~n be HBFEs, then10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathscr {D}^{-}\le {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})\le {\mathscr {D}^{+}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

Let us assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}^{-}$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma ^{-},~\eta ^{-})$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$min\{{\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n}}\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}^{+}$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma ^{+},~\eta ^{+})$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$max\{{\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n}}\}$$\end{document}, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})={\bigoplus _{t=1}^{m}}({\psi _{t}}{\mathscr {D}_{t}})= \\ & \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma ^{-}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^-}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta ^-}^2}{1-{\eta ^{-}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \\ &\le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \\ & \le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma ^{+}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^+}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta ^+}^2}{1-{\eta ^{+}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \implies \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\gamma ^{-}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^-}^2}\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\eta ^{-}}^{2}}{1-{\eta ^-}^2}\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right)\\ & \le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \\ &\le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\gamma ^{+}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^+}^2}\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\eta ^{+}}^{2}}{1-{\eta ^+}^2}\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}\right) \\ & \implies \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\gamma ^{-}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^-}^2}}}}},\sqrt{{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\eta ^{-}}^{2}}{1-{\eta ^-}^2}}}}}\right) \\ &\le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \\ & \le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\gamma ^{+}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma ^+}^2}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\frac{{\eta ^{+}}^{2}}{1-{\eta ^+}^2}}}}}}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}^{-}\le \left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right) \le \mathscr {D}^{+}$$\end{document}.

i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}^{-}\le {HBFDWA}_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})\le {\mathscr {D}^{+}}$$\end{document} □

Theorem 5

Monotonicity property: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}=(\gamma _{t},~\eta _{t})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {D}_{t}}^{\prime }=({\gamma _t}^{\prime },~{\eta _t}^{\prime })$$\end{document} be HBFEs for t=1,~2,…,~n such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}\le {\mathscr {D}_{t}}^{\prime }~ \forall ~t$$\end{document}, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} HBFDWA_{\psi }(\mathscr {D}_{1},~\mathscr {D}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {D}_{n})={HBFDWA}_{\psi }({\mathscr {D}_{1}}^{\prime },~{\mathscr {D}_{2}}^{\prime },\ldots ,~{\mathscr {D}_{n}}^{\prime }) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

Since, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{t}\le {\mathscr {D}_{t}}^{\prime }$$\end{document} i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _t\le {\gamma _{t}}^{\prime }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{t}\le {\eta _{t}}^{\prime }$$\end{document}, HBFDWAψ(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{1}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {D}_{2}$$\end{document}, … \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {D}_{n}$$\end{document}) = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \sqrt{1-{\frac{1}{{1+\{{\sum _{t=1}^{m}}\psi _{t}\left( \frac{{\gamma _{t}}^{2}}{1-{\gamma _t}^2}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}},~\sqrt{{{\frac{1}{{1+\{\sum _{t=1}^{m}\psi _{t}{\left( \frac{{\eta _t}^2}{1-{\eta _{t}}^{2}}\right) ^k\}^{1/k}}}}}}}\right)$$\end{document}. where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi =(\psi _{1},~\psi _{2},\ldots ,~\psi _{n})^T$$\end{document} be weight vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} RE sources and their implementation barriers

The shift to RE systems is an important step in meeting sustainable energy targets and mitigating the negative consequences of climate change. However, despite increasing technology improvements and regulatory backing, large-scale adoption of RE sources confronts several complex challenges54. These hurdles are not consistent among technologies; rather, they are the result of a complex interplay of economic, technological, social, institutional, and geopolitical variables. In this study, solar \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{1})$$\end{document}, wind \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{2})$$\end{document}, hydro \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{3})$$\end{document}, geothermal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{4})$$\end{document}, biomass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{5})$$\end{document}, and tidal energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S}_{6})$$\end{document} are considered as potential RE sources.

Implementation barriers of RET

The adoption of RE technology (RET) in India encounters considerable obstacles across various facets. Technical impediments, including challenges related to grid integration and insufficient storage infrastructure, obstruct the reliability and scalability of RE sources55,56. Corporate challenges include limited engagement from the private sector and the prevailing influence of traditional energy suppliers, which hinder both innovation and investment56. The presence of financial limitations, characterized by substantial initial expenditures and restricted availability of economical financing options, renders renewable projects less feasible for numerous developers57. The complexities surrounding the widespread adoption of RET throughout the nation are exacerbated by political and environmental obstacles, including inconsistent policies, challenges in land acquisition, and pressing ecological considerations58. Barriers of RET implementation in India.

Proposed hesitant bi-fuzzy MEREC-MOORA approach

This section develops an HBF decision-making framework that incorporates uncertainty and hesitancy in the MCGDM problems using the MEREC and MOORA approaches. The HBF scale captures the qualitative assessments associated with each criterion of an alternative, while the MEREC method objectively determines the corresponding criterion weights; subsequently, the HBF-extended MOORA approach facilitates a structured and systematic ranking of the options.

Proposed HBF-MOORA approach

This section outlines the methodical steps required for implementing the HBF-MOORA approach, emphasizing its ability to navigate uncertainty, ensure the reliability of weights, and yield resilient rankings of options.Step 1: MCGDM Problem: Consider a group of p DMs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}=\{\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{1},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{p}\},$$\end{document} poised to engage in an MCGDM scenario. Let there be a decision-making issue involving s alternatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}=\{\mathscr {S}_{1},~\mathscr {S}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {S}_{s}\}$$\end{document}, accompanied by t criteria \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}=\{\mathscr {B}_{1},~\mathscr {B}_{2},\ldots ,~\mathscr {B}_{t}\}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{B}$$\end{document} denotes the criteria for beneficiaries and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{NB}$$\end{document} represents the criteria for non-beneficiaries, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}=\mathscr {B}_{B} \cup \mathscr {B}_{NB}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{B} \cap \mathscr {B}_{NB}=\emptyset$$\end{document}.Step 2: HBF decision matrices: Every DM formulates their decision matrix based on the HBF rating outlined in Table 2, which is inspired and modified from Golui et al.22. Let the decision matrix for kth DM be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {R}^{k}=\left( r^{k}_{ij}\right) _{s\times t},~k=1(1)p$$\end{document}.Table 2HBF Scale for the evaluation of criteria.Linguistic termsHBFS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma ,~\Delta )$$\end{document}Highly Oppose (HO)(0.04, 0.02), (0.98, 0.94)Oppose (O)(0.25, 0.20), (0.70, 0.75)Slightly Oppose (SO)(0.30, 0.35), (0.65, 0.60)Neutral (N)(0.50, 0.50), (0.50, 0.50)Slightly Favour (SF)(0.75, 0.70), (0.25, 0.22)Favour (F)(0.90, 0.85), (0.08, 0.10)Highly Favour (HF)(0.99, 0.97), (0.05, 0.01)Step 3: CBF decision-matrix: Utilize Eq. (1) to transform the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^{th}$$\end{document} HBF decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {R}^{k}$$\end{document} into the CBF decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{X}^{k}=\left( \xi _{ij}^{k},~\zeta _{ij}^{k}\right) _{s\times t}$$\end{document}.Step 4: Credibility of DMs: Apply the rank-exponent method to determine the preference of each decision maker \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {W}_{i})$$\end{document} according to their qualifications, experience, and profession as outlined in Eq. (11). 11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathscr {W}_{k}=\frac{{(p-R_{k}+1)}^z}{\sum _{k=1}^{p}{({p-R_{k}+1})^z}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document} Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{k}$$\end{document} is the rank of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^{th}$$\end{document} DM, where the number of DMs is p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \Re$$\end{document} is the exponent value. The rank-exponent method is called the rank-sum method if z=0. Let the set of available DMs be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}=\{\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{1},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{2},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{3},~ \mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{4},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{5}\}$$\end{document}.Step 2:Each DM employs the HBF rating scale outlined in Table 2 to formulate their decision matrix, as illustrated in Table 3.Step 3:Applying Eq. (1) to the HBF decision matrices in Table 3 yields the CBF decision matrix.Step 4:Initially, we selected reputable experts in RE-related matters. The recognized experts are categorized based on their credentials, including academic qualifications, employment roles, and experience. Experience in the relevant area is expected to have a direct impact, so it is given greater significance. A rank has been awarded to the DMs based on the evaluation of the three credibility factors. The rank-sum approach (Eq. (11)) is used to determine the weights of DMs based on their ranks. The credibility and the associated preferences of the DMs are delineated in Table 4. Table 4 demonstrates that higher academic credentials and experience lead to greater importance, as it is expected that with increased experience, DMs possess deeper knowledge and better decision-making capabilities. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{5}$$\end{document} opinions are significantly more influential than the remaining DM’s opinions as he/she has maximum experience in the RE industry with a higher qualification. The weighted CBF decision matrices are generated by combining the corresponding DM weight with their CBF decision matrix as described in Step 4.Step 5:The normalized decision matrices are computed using Eq. (12).Step 6:The preferences of the barriers for each DM are derived using the MEREC approach, as outlined in Eq. (16). The results are presented in Table 5.Step 7:The suggested HBFDWA operator is employed to generate a collaborative weighted normalized decision matrix, as illustrated in Table 6. This is accomplished through the integration of the normalized decision matrices in conjunction with the criteria weights depicted in Table 5.Step 8:Table 7 presents the HBF PSs of the RE resources, calculated using Eqs. (17) and (18).Step 9:Table 7 displays the outcomes of the computation of the aggregate PSs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PV_{i}$$\end{document} using Eq. (19). We choose λ =0.5 because λ ranges between 0 and 1, making 0.5 the midpoint of this interval. This choice assigns equal preference to both options. We consider six RE sources \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}=\{\mathscr {S}_{1},~\mathscr {S}_{2},~\mathscr {S}_{3},~\mathscr {S}_{4},~\mathscr {S}_{5},~\mathscr {S}_{6}\}$$\end{document} connected to the barriers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}=\{\mathscr {B}_{11},~\mathscr {B}_{12},~\mathscr {B}_{13},~\mathscr {B}_{14},~\mathscr {B}_{15},~\mathscr {B}_{21},~\mathscr {B}_{22},~\mathscr {B}_{31},~\mathscr {B}_{32},~\mathscr {B}_{33},~\mathscr {B}_{34},~\mathscr {B}_{41},~\mathscr {B}_{42},~\mathscr {B}_{51},~\mathscr {B}_{52}\}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{NB}=\{\mathscr {B}_{31},~\mathscr {B}_{33},~\mathscr {B}_{34},~\mathscr {B}_{51},~\mathscr {B}_{52}\}$$\end{document}. Let the set of available DMs be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}=\{\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{1},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{2},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{3},~ \mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{4},~\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{5}\}$$\end{document}.

Each DM employs the HBF rating scale outlined in Table 2 to formulate their decision matrix, as illustrated in Table 3.

Applying Eq. (1) to the HBF decision matrices in Table 3 yields the CBF decision matrix.

Initially, we selected reputable experts in RE-related matters. The recognized experts are categorized based on their credentials, including academic qualifications, employment roles, and experience. Experience in the relevant area is expected to have a direct impact, so it is given greater significance. A rank has been awarded to the DMs based on the evaluation of the three credibility factors. The rank-sum approach (Eq. (11)) is used to determine the weights of DMs based on their ranks. The credibility and the associated preferences of the DMs are delineated in Table 4. Table 4 demonstrates that higher academic credentials and experience lead to greater importance, as it is expected that with increased experience, DMs possess deeper knowledge and better decision-making capabilities. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr{D}\mathscr{M}_{5}$$\end{document} opinions are significantly more influential than the remaining DM’s opinions as he/she has maximum experience in the RE industry with a higher qualification. The weighted CBF decision matrices are generated by combining the corresponding DM weight with their CBF decision matrix as described in Step 4.

The normalized decision matrices are computed using Eq. (12).

The preferences of the barriers for each DM are derived using the MEREC approach, as outlined in Eq. (16). The results are presented in Table 5.

The suggested HBFDWA operator is employed to generate a collaborative weighted normalized decision matrix, as illustrated in Table 6. This is accomplished through the integration of the normalized decision matrices in conjunction with the criteria weights depicted in Table 5.

Table 7 presents the HBF PSs of the RE resources, calculated using Eqs. (17) and (18).

Table 7 displays the outcomes of the computation of the aggregate PSs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PV_{i}$$\end{document} using Eq. (19). We choose λ =0.5 because λ ranges between 0 and 1, making 0.5 the midpoint of this interval. This choice assigns equal preference to both options.

Comparison study

It is essential to align the results of the proposed HBF-MOORA with those of existing classical MCDM techniques to validate it. The HBF decision matrix (Table 6) is transformed into a crisp format using Eq. (2) prior to implementing these MCDM methodologies. Comparison of PSs and ranking orders of the RE sources.

From Table 9, it is evident that hydro energy consistently ranks higher across the majority of techniques. It occupies the first position in TOPSIS, MARICA, MARCOS, and PF-MOORA, and the second and fifth positions in EDAS and HBF-MOORA, respectively. This implies strong consensus on the superiority of hydro energy, particularly in methods that rely on distance- or normalization-based aggregation (e.g., TOPSIS, MARICA). In contrast to the evaluations of other techniques, the proposed HBF-MOORA approach identifies solar as the most preferred alternative. Notably, HBF-MOORA allocates solar as the first classification, whereas EDAS and MARICA place it fifth. This deviation highlights the impact of the underlying logic and scale in HBF-MOORA, which incorporates hesitant behavior through a fuzzy scale and produces a more nuanced evaluation of criterion interactions. In several models, wind energy has emerged as a formidable competitor, securing second place in MARCOS, CODAS, and PF-MOORA, first place in MARICA, and mid- and lower-level rankings in others. Conversely, geothermal energy exhibits a consistent pattern of underperformance, ranking sixth in EDAS, TOPSIS, MARCOS, CODAS, PF-MOORA, and HBF-MOORA. This consistency bolsters the dependability of evaluation metrics in identifying less desirable alternatives. Furthermore, the classification of biomass energy exhibits substantial variation, with it ranging from second (EDAS) to sixth (MARICA), indicating potential sensitivity to specific criterion weighting or scaling methodologies. In the same vein, tidal energy is in the middle of the pack across most methodologies, suggesting it is moderately suitable but lacks strong consensus.

The analysis emphasizes that while specific methodologies, such as MARCOS and EDAS, produce closely aligned rankings, techniques like HBF-MOORA offer a distinctive perspective by capturing qualitative hesitancy during the evaluation process. These two methods have top-ranked nearly identical alternatives; however, there are slight variations in the middle- and lower-ranked alternatives. The observed divergences underscore the importance of implementing ensemble or hybrid approaches in selecting RE sources, thereby ensuring a more comprehensive and resilient decision-making framework. The rank correlation between the approaches is shown in Table 10 to elucidate their relationship.Table 10Spearman rank correlation matrix among comparison MCDM methodologies.MethodTOPSISMARICAMARCOSCODASPF-MOORAHBF-MOORAEDAS0.5430.7710.8290.6570.943− 0.086TOPSIS0.4860.6570.8290.6000.429MARICA0.9430.5430.829− 0.086MARCOS0.6000.886− 0.029CODAS0.7140.143PF-MOORA− 0.086

Table 10Spearman rank correlation matrix among comparison MCDM methodologies.MethodTOPSISMARICAMARCOSCODASPF-MOORAHBF-MOORAEDAS0.5430.7710.8290.6570.943− 0.086TOPSIS0.4860.6570.8290.6000.429MARICA0.9430.5430.829− 0.086MARCOS0.6000.886− 0.029CODAS0.7140.143PF-MOORA− 0.086

Spearman rank correlation matrix among comparison MCDM methodologies.

According to Table 10, the proposed HBF-MOORA exhibits a distinct ranking in comparison to the majority of the existing methodologies. It only demonstrates a moderate positive correlation with TOPSIS and a very low positive correlation with CODAS. HBF-MOORA’s ranking-order divergence underscores its capacity to retain bipolar hesitant information throughout the process. The correlation analysis provides evidence of the non-redundant ranking with existing methodologies, demonstrating the methodological uniqueness of the approach in managing hesitant information. Figure 2 is drawn to illustrate the variation in score values among these approaches.Fig. 2Performance comparison of RE sources using various techniques.

Performance comparison of RE sources using various techniques.

Figure 2 illustrates PS lines that highlight the significant variation in PSs across different methods, underscoring the impact of the underlying computational logic and decision criteria on the evaluation results. MARICA demonstrates the most extensive score dispersion, particularly in the context of wind and hydropower energy sources. This suggests that MARICA is either highly sensitive to input variations or may allocate dominant weights to specific criteria, exacerbating the performance disparities between alternatives. CODAS also exhibits significant divergence, albeit to a lesser extent than MARICA, particularly in biomass and tidal energy. Conversely, the proposed HBF-MOORA, PF-MOORA, MARCOS, TOPSIS, and EDAS exhibit relatively stable PS trajectories, indicating that the evaluation dynamics are more conservative or balanced. The HBF-MOORA displays a comparatively moderate profile, with minimal fluctuation among the alternatives, suggesting its ability to integrate qualitative (hesitant, imprecise behaviour) and quantitative assessments. In particular, it prioritizes solar and tidal energy over other methodologies. In MARICA and PF-MOORA, the intersection points between lines (e.g., Solar vs. Wind) indicate potential rank reversals under various methods, thereby underscoring the necessity of multi-method consensus or ensemble approaches.

This graphical representation demonstrates the significance of method selection in evaluating MCDM-based RE. Some techniques, such as MARICA, prioritize discrimination, whereas others, like HBF-MOORA, ensure decision stability, providing policymakers with a more confident framework for selecting sustainable energy solutions.

Sensitivity analysis

The outcome of the proposed HBF-MOORA is analyzed through a set of sensitivity analyses in this section. To explore the alternate decision, we have presented three types of parameter variation in the sensitivity analysis: choice parameter variation, ranking alteration of DMs, and criteria weight variation.

Choice parameter variation

Nine variations in the choice parameter (λ ) were established to evaluate the impact on the ranking sequences of the RE sources. However, at λ =0.5, it occupies the sixth position. The biomass energy consistently occupies the first and sixth positions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in [0.1,~0.4]$$\end{document} and [0.6, 0.9], except λ =0.5, where it occupies the fourth position. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in [0.1,~0.4]$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in [0.6,~0.9]$$\end{document}, the tidal energy occupies the third and fourth positions, respectively. However, at λ =0.5, it occupies the second position. The decision expert may assign nearly equal importance to membership and non-membership of PSs, since it can substantially impact the ranking order of the RE sources. Figure 3 demonstrates the effect of variations in the choice parameter on the ranking orders of the RE sources.Fig. 3Impact on PSs on choice parameter variation.

Impact on PSs on choice parameter variation.

As illustrated in Fig. 3, the PSs of RE sources exhibit fluctuations when the choice parameter is near 0.1 or 0.9. The PSs exhibit the slightest variation at λ =0.5. i.e., the PSs of the RE source respond almost identically if the DM allocates equal importance to both grades. In summary, solar, wind, and hydro energy are the more advantageous RE sources if the DM assigns a higher priority to the membership component of the PS. If the DM favors the non-membership component of the PS, the RE sources, such as geothermal, biomass, and tidal energy, are the most preferred.

Criteria weight variation

The variation in criteria weights across various DMs is a critical factor in assuring a robust and representative evaluation process in an MCGDM problem. The primary method for determining the criteria weights in the proposed HBF-MOORA framework is the MEREC. The CRITIC method67 and the rank-exponent method (Eq. (11)) are utilized to ascertain the criteria preference. The proposed HBF-MOORA approach has been implemented to determine the ranking orders of RE sources after determining the weights of the criteria. Impact of the weight determination procedures on the ranking of RE sources.

Table 12 shows solar energy proves its superiority among the remaining RE sources throughout all weight determination approaches. It is noticeable that the difference in PSs between the first and second positions decreases when the exponent z increases. Even the range of PSs significantly diminishes for the same RE sources when z increases, but not the ranking positions. Also, the range of PSs in MEREC is significantly more extensive than in the rank exponent approaches for different z values. Therefore, the decision expert ranks the RE sources more conveniently in the MEREC approach compared to the remaining procedures. The performance of the RE sources, as determined by the criteria weight procedures, is displayed in Fig. 4.Fig. 4Impact of criteria weights determination on PSs of RE sources.

Impact of criteria weights determination on PSs of RE sources.

The PSs of all RE sources gradually decrease as z increases, approaching the rank exponent. The PS of solar energy in MEREC is the highest, while the PS of tidal energy is the lowest for the rank exponent approach (z=0). Solar and geothermal energy compete closely to acquire the first position in all criteria weight determination procedures. There is a close fight for fifth position between the RE sources of hydro and biomass energies. Overall, Fig. 4 shows an eagle-eye view of the performance of RE sources over the various criteria for weight determination.

DM’s preference variation

The variation of DMs’ preferences may significantly influence the performance of alternatives in MCDM problems, as they are the primary source of initial information concerning the barriers of the RE sources. The reliability of the DMs is assessed by analyzing three instances related to their qualifications, experience, and designations through the rank-exponent method. Influence of fluctuations in DMs’ weights on the hierarchy of RE sources.

Table 14 demonstrates a notable influence of DM’s preference on the ranking orders of RE sources, although the leading option remains identical. The final place is occupied by either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}_{3}$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}_{4}$$\end{document}, proving their inferiority to the remaining RE sources. The range of the PSs is higher in the impact of experience, and here, the RE source \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}_{1}$$\end{document} is at the top position in all ranking orders. The impact of experience, qualification, and occupation on the ranking orders for the six RE sources is shown in Fig. 5.Fig. 5Variations in rankings of RE sources across diverse scenarios of DMs’ weight.

Variations in rankings of RE sources across diverse scenarios of DMs’ weight.

Figure 5 illustrates the variations in the rankings of RE sources under various scenarios of DMs’ weightings derived from three main influencing factors: experience, qualification, and occupation. The plotted curves indicate substantial variations in ranking patterns contingent upon the DMs’ backgrounds. For example, geothermal energy is more highly ranked when professional experience is the primary factor (blue line). In contrast, wind and hydropower are more highly ranked when qualification (red line) and occupation (yellow line) are the determining factors. This divergence indicates the impact of the perspectives and priorities of DMs on the evaluation outcome, particularly when preferences are translated into weight distributions in MCGDM models.

Conclusion and future research

This study proposes a novel integration of the MEREC and MOORA methods within an HBF framework to address uncertainties in MCDM for RE planning. By extending the Dombi aggregation operator into the HBF environment, the framework enables a more balanced and transparent synthesis of expert judgments, improving the reliability of RE source ranking under conflicting sustainability criteria. A numerical illustration of RE source selection demonstrates the robustness of the approach, with comparative and sensitivity analyses confirming its validity against established MCDM techniques.

The proposed framework advances assessment by offering (i) refined evaluation of ecological, economic, and technical criteria through HBF measures, (ii) objective weight determination for each expert, (iii) a fair aggregation process that accounts for hesitation and uncertainty, and (iv) improved prioritization of RE sources that directly support clean energy adoption and pollution reduction strategies. In doing so, it provides valuable insights for policymakers, industries, and researchers engaged in accelerating the transition to RE while ensuring environmental protection.

Nevertheless, the current work is bounded by certain limitations, which can be improved through the implementation of (i) crisp weight determination in the MEREC process may limit flexibility in highly uncertain contexts which can be improved through the HBF extension, (ii) future studies should extend the model to include diverse RE technologies, market dynamics, and climate-related factors, while also advancing the uncertainty modeling using Z-numbers, D-numbers, or type-II fuzzy sets, (iii) further exploration of alternative MCDM approaches, such as TOPSIS, CODAS, or MABAC, within the HBF setting could enhance decision robustness, and (iv) integration of deep learning and adaptive AI68,69, spatial feature fusion, and real-time data integration improves the proposed framework’s accuracy, scalability, and automation. By embedding these innovations into environmental decision-making, the proposed methodology has the potential to significantly advance green development goals and reduce environmental pollution through informed, sustainable energy planning.
